## Examining the Scriptures Daily

Eating and drinking, going to work, going to school, talking to friends, watching TV, sleeping—these are among the things that most people do just about every day. Perhaps that is true of you as well. For many, though, these things appear to be all that they do. Indeed, most people go through their day without ever giving thought to God or what God expects of them.

For Christians, however, there is another dimension to their life. They realize that for life to have meaning and direction, they must do more than simply satisfy their physical needs. They recognize the truism in Jesus' words: 'Man must live, not on bread alone, but on every utterance coming forth through Jehovah's mouth.' —Matt. 4:4.

For us to benefit from Jehovah's every utterance, of course, many things are involved. But what better way is there than to set aside some time each day to consider a portion of God's Word and how it applies in our lives? The first-century Christians in the Macedonian city of Beroea were commended for being 'noble-minded' because they had the habit of 'examining the Scriptures daily' in order to be certain that the things they had heard and learned were indeed true. (Acts 17:11) We need to do the same today so that we can have 'the prophetic word made more sure' in our hearts and in our minds.—2 Pet. 1:19.

It is for this purpose that this booklet is published. For each day of the year, a Bible text is selected for consideration. Further, an excerpt is taken from the pages of the issue of _The Watchtower_ that enlarges upon the meaning of the text or that comments on the application

3

---

<sup>**Page 4**</sup>

[Open Page 4 of PDF](https://pdfs-noindex.themindscapeishere.workers.dev/1998_es_E.pdf#page=4)

of it. Reading the text and the comments each day will do much toward setting and keeping our hearts and minds on a spiritual plane.

Wherever possible, it is best to consider the text in the morning when our mind is fresh and unencumbered by the worries and anxieties of the day. If the whole family can do so together each morning, that is most desirable. To be sure, it might require each member of the family to get up a little earlier, but it is well worth the effort. The psalmist expressed it this way: 'In the morning cause me to hear your loving-kindness, for in you I have put my trust. Make known to me the way in which I should walk, for to you I have lifted up my soul.' (Ps. 143:8) How wonderful each morning to be refreshed by hearing about the loving-kindness of Jehovah and to be reminded of the way in which we should walk!

If your circumstances are such that it is not possible for you to consider the daily text in the morning, then by all means set a definite time during the day to do so, making it part of your daily routine. Just as a regular eating habit leads to good physical health, a regular habit of considering the daily text and comments will result in good spiritual health.

An in-depth discussion of the daily text is a daily routine at Bethel and in missionary homes. Individuals who have, for various reasons, found it necessary to leave Bethel service often observe that they sorely miss that daily provision. Granted, time and effort is needed to prepare if we are to benefit from the daily text discussion as members of the Bethel family do. But if families and individuals do what they can consistently, they will find not only that their Scriptural knowledge will steadily increase but also that their thinking, actions, and reactions will be more in line with the Bible. As the psalmist declared, 'the law of Jehovah is perfect, bringing back the soul. The reminder of Jehovah is trustworthy, making the inexperienced one wise.'—Ps. 19:7.

4

## 1998 Yeartext

### 'Everyone who calls on the name of Jehovah will be saved.'

—Rom. 10:13.

Jehovah! That should be the name dearest to us, the name that we want to cherish in our hearts and carry on our lips all day long. But how many people today call on God's name? In fact, we might ask, how many people today _know_ God's name? Very few—less than one in a thousand on a global scale.

Why, though, should we call on Jehovah's name? As the text for 1998 indicates, our salvation is involved. That is certainly reason enough for us to call on the name of Jehovah. When the apostle Paul wrote the above to the Christians in Rome, he was quoting the prophet Joel, who spoke about 'the coming of the great and fear-inspiring day of Jehovah' and gave the admonition to call on Jehovah's name for salvation. (Joel 2:30-32) Those words ring out with even greater urgency today. But what does it mean to call on the name of Jehovah? And how does one do so?

Our calling on the name of Jehovah requires, of course, that first of all we know that name. We do not worship a nameless God, an unknown entity or being. We feel the same as the psalmist did when he prayed 'that people may know that you, whose name is Jehovah, you alone are the Most High over all the earth.'—Ps. 83:18.

To call on the name of Jehovah, however, means more than just to know God's name, or even to invoke it in a ritualistic or ceremonial way. To call on Jehovah's name means to trust in and rely on him. For example, challenging the Baal worshipers on Mount Carmel, Elijah said to them: 'You must call upon the name of your god, and I, for my part, shall call upon the name of Jehovah.' How did the Baal worshipers call on their god? The account tells us: 'They kept calling upon the name of Baal from morning till noon, saying: 'O Baal, answer us!' But there was no voice, and there was no one answering.' Even though they knew the name of their god, their calling on that name was futile. When Elijah's turn came, he poured out his heart in a fervent prayer to the true God, Jehovah, expressing his deep concern about the sanctification

5

---

<sup>**Page 5**</sup>

[Open Page 5 of PDF](https://pdfs-noindex.themindscapeishere.workers.dev/1998_es_E.pdf#page=5)

of God's name and the accomplishing of His will. The result? Jehovah answered him favorably.—1 Ki. 18:24, 26, 36-38.

What made the difference? For one thing, the Baal worshippers were calling on a god that did not exist. Their idol-gods and goddesses were in reality not gods at all. (Ps. 115: 4-8) In addition, they called on their god simply for their own selfish advantage. Today many people are like that. They turn to gods that are not gods at all. (1 Cor. 8:5, 6) And they call on their gods only when they want something from them. Is there any sound reason that such ones should expect an answer from their gods?

Elijah, on the other hand, was known for his faith and his courage in doing God's will. He called on Jehovah numerous times in his long career of sacred service and was favorably heard. With good reason, therefore, James cited him as an example worthy of our imitation.—Jas. 5:17, 18.

From our brief consideration, we can see why it is important, yes, vital, for us to call on the name of Jehovah daily. It means our salvation to life! But what about our neighbors who do not know God's name? As the apostle Paul says, 'how will they call on him in whom they have not put faith? How, in turn, will they put faith in him of whom they have not heard? How, in turn, will they hear without someone to preach?' (Rom. 10:14) Will you answer those questions by saying as Isaiah did, 'Here I am! Send me'?—Isa. 6:8.

Realizing that we are greatly indebted to our God, Jehovah, in everything, we should resolve to do as the psalmist who said: 'What shall I repay to Jehovah for all his benefits to me? The cup of grand salvation I shall take up, and on the name of Jehovah I shall call.' (Ps. 116:12, 13) May we demonstrate our trust and confidence in Jehovah by regularly calling on his name in faith, for truly, 'everyone who calls on the name of Jehovah will be saved.'

### DAILY TEXTS AND COMMENTS

There is a text for each day and a comment on that text. The comments are taken from the _Watchtower_ (w) issues of 1996-97. Figures following the date of the _Watchtower_ issue refer to paragraphs in the first study article, where further comment on the text may be found. When 'a' follows the paragraph number, the comment is found in the second study article; when 'b' is shown, it refers to the third study article.

6

### Thursday, January 1

_A man of intrigues keeps sending forth contention, and a slanderer is separating those familiar with one another._

—Prov. 16:28.

Slander is one of Satan's specialties, and he tries to use it in the congregation. His favorite method is gossip. Do we allow ourselves to be his dupes in this unholy conduct? How could that be? By initiating it, by repeating it, or by listening to it. (1 Tim. 5:13) What is the antidote to gossip and slander? We should make sure that our speech is always upbuilding and based on love. If we look for the virtues rather than the supposed vices of our brothers, our conversation will always be pleasant and spiritual. The apostle Paul wrote: 'As God's chosen ones, holy and loved, clothe yourselves with the tender affections of compassion, kindness, lowliness of mind, mildness, and long-suffering. Continue putting up with one another and forgiving one another freely.' (Col. 3:12, 13) With this forgiving spirit, we can maintain the unity and the holiness of the congregation. w 8/1/96 14, 15a

### Friday, January 2

_Paul, a slave of God and an apostle of Jesus Christ . . . , to Titus, a genuine child according to a faith shared in common._

—Titus 1:1, 4.

The Greek Christian named Titus was among those who rendered a good account of themselves. It has been suggested that he became a Christian during Paul's first missionary journey to Cyprus. Since Jews and proselytes from Cyprus may have been in Jerusalem during Pentecost of 33 C.E., Christianity may have reached the island shortly

thereafter. (Acts 11:19) Nevertheless, Titus proved to be one of Paul's faithful fellow workers. He accompanied Paul and Barnabas on the journey to Jerusalem about 49 C.E., when the vital issue of circumcision was resolved. The fact that Titus was uncircumcised added weight to Paul's argument that converts to Christianity should not be under the Mosaic Law. (Gal. 2:1-3) The fine ministry of Titus is attested to in the Scriptures, and Paul even directed a divinely inspired letter to him. Evidently to the very end of his earthly course, Titus continued to render a fine account of himself to God. w 9/15/96 16

### Saturday, January 3

_Let us hold fast the public declaration of our hope without wavering, for he is faithful that promised._—Heb. 10:23.

Sessions with your little ones can be used to prepare them to share the water of truth with others, such as by commenting at meetings. 'During our practice sessions, I would have to comment in my own words,' one youth remembers. 'I was not permitted just to read without understanding.' In addition, children can be trained to have a meaningful share in the field ministry. A woman reared by God-fearing parents explains: 'We were never tagalongs who merely accompanied our parents in their work. We knew we had a share, even if it was only to ring a doorbell and leave a handbill. By careful preparation prior to each weekend's activities, we knew what we would say. We never woke up on a Saturday morning asking if we were going in the ministry. We knew we were.' The need regularly to supply little ones with the water of Bible truth cannot be overemphasized. w 12/1/96 15, 16

## Sunday, January 4

_He has told you, O earthling man, what is good. And what is Jehovah asking back from you but to exercise justice and to love kindness and to be modest in walking with your God?_

—Mic. 6:8.

What is Jehovah asking back from you? In this day of individuality and independence, many are uncomfortable with the idea that God requires something of them. They do not want to be obliged. But what of the conclusion that Solomon reached in Ecclesiastes? “The conclusion of the matter, everything having been heard, is: Fear the true God and keep his commandments. For this is the whole obligation of man.” (Eccl. 12:13) We can be greatly benefited if we consider the background of that conclusion. King Solomon, the writer of this inspired book, considered some of the very things that are part of our day-to-day life. Some might hastily conclude that his analysis is basically negative. Yet it was divinely inspired and can help us to evaluate our activities and priorities, with increased joy as a result. _w 2/15/97_ 1-3a

## Monday, January 5

_To this course you were called, because even Christ suffered for you, leaving you a model for you to follow his steps closely._

—1 Pet. 2:21.

For clues as to what is paramount in life, we can examine what Jesus Christ thought about it. Psalm 40:8 said prophetically of him: “To do your will, O my God, I have delighted, and your law is within my inward parts.” This is how he stated the same thought: “My food is for me to do the will of him that sent

me and to finish his work.” (John 4:34) Note that key—the doing of God’s will. Jesus’ example emphasizes what his disciples should rightly make paramount in their lives, for he said that “everyone that is perfectly instructed will be like his teacher.” (Luke 6:40) And Jesus showed that there was “rejoicing to satisfaction” in keeping God’s will paramount. (Ps. 16:11; Acts 2:28) Likewise, as we choose the doing of God’s will as the paramount thing in our lives, we will enjoy “rejoicing to satisfaction” and real life. —1 Tim. 6:19. _w 12/15/96_ 5, 6

## Tuesday, January 6

_God loved the world so much that he gave his only-begotten Son, in order that everyone exercising faith in him might not be destroyed but have everlasting life._—John 3:16.

“Exercising faith” involves living by the law of the Christ. That includes obeying all of Christ’s commands. Does this mean committing to memory hundreds of laws and statutes? No. While Moses, the mediator of the old covenant, wrote down the Mosaic Law, Jesus, the Mediator of the new covenant, never wrote down a single law. Instead, he lived this law and laid down a pattern for all to follow. (1 Pet. 2:21) Perhaps that is why the early Christians’ worship was referred to as “The Way.” (Acts 9:2; 19:9, 23; 22:4; 24:22) To them, the law of the Christ was exemplified in the life of the Christ. To imitate Jesus was to obey this law. Their intense love of him meant that this law was indeed written on their hearts, as prophesied. (Jer. 31:33; 1 Pet. 4:8) And one who is obedient because of love never feels oppressed—a good reason why the law of the Christ may be called “the law of a free people.” —Jas. 2:12. _w 9/1/96_ 7, 8a

## Wednesday, January 7

_Jesus and his disciples were also invited to the marriage feast._ —John 2:2.

Jesus attended a marriage feast in Cana and contributed to the joy of the occasion by performing his first miracle there. (John 2:1-11) Likewise today, Jehovah’s people have happy times together on similar special occasions, and appropriate celebration and festivity add much to such events. What, though, is appropriate? From our study of the Bible, we learn what is appropriate conduct for Christians, and this we follow at all times. (Rom. 13:12-14; Gal. 5:19-21; Eph. 5:3-5) Social gatherings, whether in connection with weddings or for any other reason, do not give us license to abandon our Christian standards or to do something that we would not ordinarily do; nor are we obligated to follow all the customs of the land in which we live. Never forget, many of such are based on false religious practices or superstitions, and others involve conduct clearly unacceptable to Christians.—1 Pet. 4:3, 4. _w 10/1/96_ 15, 16a

## Thursday, January 8

_You husbands, continue dwelling in like manner with them according to knowledge, assigning them honor as to a weaker vessel, the feminine one, since you are also heirs with them of the undeserved favor of life, in order for your prayers not to be hindered._—1 Pet. 3:7.

Just as elders make sacrifices so as to benefit others, many wives of elders have striven to balance their responsibilities in marriage with vital Kingdom interests. Thou-

sands of Christian women are happy to cooperate to enable their husbands to carry out their duties as overseers. Jehovah loves them for this, and he blesses the fine spirit they show. (Philem. 25) Nevertheless, Paul’s balanced counsel shows that wives of overseers can legitimately expect a reasonable amount of time and attention from their husbands. It is the Scriptural duty of married elders to devote adequate time to their wives so as to balance their responsibilities as husband and overseer. _w 10/15/96_ 15, 20a

## Friday, January 9

_What will be the sign of your presence and of the conclusion of the system of things?_

—Matt. 24:3.

In view of what Jesus said about the temple, the disciples likely were thinking of the Jewish arrangement when they asked the above. At this point the apostles had but a limited grasp of Jesus’ teachings. They had earlier imagined that “the kingdom of God was going to display itself instantly.” (Luke 19:11) And even after the discussion on the Mount of Olives, but prior to being anointed with holy spirit, they asked if Jesus was restoring the Kingdom to Israel then. (Acts 1:6) Yet, they did know that he would leave. (John 12:35; 14:28) So they might well have wondered, “If Jesus is going to leave, how will we recognize his return?” When he appeared as the Messiah, most did not recognize him. And over a year later, questions persisted about whether he would fulfill all that the Messiah was to do. (Matt. 11:2, 3) So the apostles had reason to inquire about the future. _w 8/15/96_ 5-7

## Saturday, January 10

_As you have accepted Christ Jesus the Lord, go on walking in union with him, rooted and being built up in him and being stabilized in the faith, just as you were taught, overflowing with faith in thanksgiving.—Col. 2:6, 7._

Traveling overseers want to strengthen the congregations and build them up spiritually. (Acts 15:41) They work hard to give encouragement, provide refreshment, and promote a desire to fulfill the ministry and continue living the truth. They also seek to stabilize fellow believers in the faith. Remember that the traveling overseer is a 'genuine yokefellow,' not a master over the faith of others. (Phil. 4:3; 2 Cor. 1:24) His visit is an occasion for encouragement and extra activity, as well as an opportunity for the body of elders to review progress made and consider future goals. By his words and example, all in the congregation can expect to be built up and stimulated for the work ahead. So, then, wholeheartedly support the circuit overseer's visits, and take full advantage of the service rendered by the district overseer. _w 11/15/96_ 18a

## Sunday, January 11

_Jerusalem will be trampled on by the nations, until the appointed times of the nations are fulfilled.—Luke 21:24._

In the 1870's, a group of sincere Christians began to engage in a penetrating study of the Bible. From Scripture, they were able to pinpoint the year 1914 as marking the conclusion of 'the appointed times of the nations.' It was then that seven symbolic 'times' (2,520 years of beastlike human rule) ended with the heavenly enthronement of Christ Jesus—the One having 'the

legal right' as earth's Messianic King. (Dan. 4:25; Ezek. 21:26, 27) Particularly from 1919 onward, these Bible Students, known today as Jehovah's Witnesses, have been engaged in vigorously spreading throughout the earth the good news of the incoming Kingdom. They increased in number down to the year 1935 when 56,153 reported field service. In that year, 52,465 had partaken of the emblems of bread and wine at the annual Memorial of Jesus' death, thus symbolizing their hope of becoming priests with Christ Jesus in the heavenly part of Jehovah's great spiritual temple.—Rom. 8:15-17. _w 1/1/97_ 15

## Monday, January 12

_By this all will know that you are my disciples, if you have love among yourselves.—John 13:35._

That is a love that transcends the national, social, economic, and racial boundaries now dividing the rest of humankind. Having observed this, millions of people earth wide are saying to Jehovah's anointed servants: 'We will go with you people, for we have heard that God is with you people.' (Zech. 8:23) As Jehovah's people, we rejoice greatly in what has been accomplished, but the work is not yet done. After sowing seed and cultivating his field, a farmer does not quit. He keeps on working, especially at the height of the harvest season. And right now there is a greater harvest of worshipers of the true God than ever before. This is a time of rejoicing. (Isa. 9:3) True, we encounter opposition and indifference. As individuals, we may be endeavoring to deal with serious illness, difficult family situations, or economic hardship. But love for Jehovah impels us to persevere. The message that has been entrusted to us by God is something that people need to hear. _w 1/15/97_ 21, 22

## Tuesday, January 13

_He will certainly become like a tree planted by streams of water, that gives its own fruit in its season and the foliage of which does not wither, and everything he does will succeed.—Ps. 1:3._

Saplings particularly need a constant water supply, as is indicated by how well trees grow beside a river. Babes too will flourish spiritually if they are regularly provided the water of Bible truth. But parents need to consider their child's attention span. Perhaps frequent abbreviated sessions of instruction would be more effective than a few long ones. Spending time together is vital to create a bonding between parent and child, a closeness repeatedly encouraged in the Scriptures. (Deut. 11:18-21; Prov. 22:6) One of the sessions with the little ones can be at the close of the day. One youth remembers: 'My parents would sit on the end of our bed every night and listen to us say our own prayers.' When children _daily_ hear their parents speak about Jehovah and pray to him, he becomes a real person to them. _w 12/1/96_ 11, 12

## Wednesday, January 14

_A name is better than good oil, and the day of death than the day of one's being born.—Eccl. 7:1._

The day of death can be better than the day of one's being born, if one has by then built up a good name with Jehovah, who can resurrect faithful ones who die. The Greater Solomon, Jesus Christ, proved that. For instance, he raised back to life the faithful man Lazarus. (Luke 11:31; John 11:1-44) As you can imagine, many of those who witnessed Lazarus' coming back

to life were greatly affected, putting faith in God's Son. (John 11:45) Do you think that they felt without purpose in life, having no idea who they were and where they were going? On the contrary, they could see that they need not be mere animals that are born, live for a time, and then die. Their purpose in life was directly and intimately tied up with knowing Jesus' Father and doing His will. Yet, having genuine and meaningful purpose in living means far more than thinking about death and living again thereafter. It involves what we are doing with our lives on a day-to-day basis. _w 2/15/97_ 17, 20, 21

## Thursday, January 15

_Teach me to do your will, for you are my God. Your spirit is good; may it lead me in the land of uprightness.—Ps. 143:10._

Material in the weekly congregation _Watchtower_ Study frequently deals with the application of Bible principles. This certainly helps us to cope with life's anxieties. In other studies profound spiritual truths or Bible prophecies are considered. How much we are taught during such studies! In many lands Kingdom Halls are filled to capacity for these meetings. Yet in a number of countries, meeting attendance has declined. Why, do you think? Might some be allowing secular work to interfere with their assembling together regularly to be taught by Jehovah? Or might many hours be spent in social activities or in watching television, making the schedule seem too tight to get to all the meetings? Recall the inspired injunction at Hebrews 10:23-25. Is not gathering together for divine instruction even more important now as we 'behold the day drawing near'? _w 12/15/96_ 8a

## Friday, January 16

_Jesus performed \[the miracle] in Cana of Galilee as the beginning of his signs, and he made his glory manifest; and his disciples put their faith in him.—John 2:11._

Reading John 2:1-11, it is not difficult for us to see that the wedding feast in Cana was an elaborate one and that quite a large number of guests were there. However, it was also well organized and properly supervised. The account closes by stating that by what he did at the feast, Jesus 'made his glory manifest.' Would he have chosen that occasion to do that had it been a rowdy and wild party? Undoubtedly not. What, then, about any special occasions that we may host? We want to remember that the purpose of receiving others with hospitality is that we might all 'become fellow workers in the truth.' (3 John 8) Thus, it is not enough to label an event a 'Witness' gathering. The question may be asked, Is it in fact a witness to who we are and what we believe? These occasions must properly reflect our role as Jehovah's Witnesses, and we should be sure that what we do brings glory and honor to Jehovah.—Matt. 5:16; 1 Cor. 10:31-33. _w 10/1/96_ 17, 18a

## Saturday, January 17

_If one knows how to do what is right and yet does not do it, it is a sin for him.—Jas. 4:17._

Jesus gave his followers this commission: 'Go . . . and make disciples of people of all the nations, baptizing them . . . , teaching them to observe all the things I have commanded you.' (Matt. 28:19, 20) The desire to observe all the things Jesus had commanded would come from the heart. And the deeper our Scriptural knowledge is, the better is the foundation for our faith. At

the same time, our accountability to God becomes greater. The disciple James' statement above is evidently a conclusion to what he had just said about boasting instead of completely depending on God. If a person knows that he can accomplish nothing lasting without Jehovah's help but does not act accordingly, this is a sin. But James' words can also apply to sins of omission. In Jesus' parable of the sheep and the goats, for instance, the goats are condemned, not for bad acts, but for not helping Christ's brothers.—Matt. 25:41-46. _w 9/15/96_ 4, 5a

## Sunday, January 18

_We do not wish you to be ignorant, brothers, about the tribulation that happened to us in the district of Asia, that we were under extreme pressure beyond our strength, so that we were very uncertain even of our lives._

—2 Cor. 1:8.

Many present-day Christians can relate to the tribulations suffered by Paul. (2 Cor. 11:23-27) Today, too, Christians have been 'under extreme pressure beyond \[their] strength,' and many have faced situations in which they 'were very uncertain of their lives.' Some have died at the hands of mass murderers and cruel persecutors. We can be sure that God's comforting power enabled them to endure and that they died with hearts and minds firmly fixed on the fulfillment of their hope, be that a heavenly hope or an earthly one. (1 Cor. 10:13; Phil. 4:13; Rev. 2:10) In other cases, Jehovah has maneuvered matters, and our brothers have been rescued from death. No doubt those who have undergone such a rescue have developed increased trust 'in the God who raises up the dead.'—2 Cor. 1:9. _w 11/1/96_ 16a

## Monday, January 19

_Go on carrying the burdens of one another, and thus fulfill the law of the Christ.—Gal. 6:2._

Some who have visited Bethel homes around the world say that such are good examples of balance in the matter of rules for a family. Though composed of adults, such institutions function much like families. Bethel operations are complex and require a fair number of rules—certainly more than the average family. Nevertheless, such homes are not like Christendom's monasteries. There are no 'abbots,' or 'fathers,' in that sense. (Matt. 23:9) The elders taking the lead in Bethel homes, offices, and factory operations endeavor to apply the law of the Christ. They view it as their assignment not only to organize the work but also to promote spiritual progress and 'the joy of Jehovah' among their fellow workers. (Neh. 8:10) Therefore, they endeavor to do things in a positive and encouraging way and strive to be reasonable. (Eph. 4:31, 32) No wonder Bethel families are known for their joyful spirit! _w 9/1/96_ 7b

## Tuesday, January 20

_Keep on the watch and pray continually.—Matt. 26:41._

Prayer was an important part of Jesus' life. When the time came for him to select his apostles, Jesus did not simply rely on his own judgment, though formerly all the angels in heaven were under his supervision. Humbly he spent an entire night in prayer to his Father. (Luke 6:12, 13) When he faced arrest and a painful death, Jesus again turned to his Father, praying earnestly. He did not take the view that he knew Satan well and could easily handle whatever that wicked one might devise. When suffer-

ing on the torture stake, he prayed in behalf of those of his taunters who were acting in ignorance: 'Father, forgive them, for they do not know what they are doing.' (Luke 23:34) He kept his mind focused on his Father, 'committing himself to the one who judges righteously.' The final words that he uttered on the torture stake were a prayer to his Father. (1 Pet. 2:23; Luke 23:46) How grateful we are that, with full reliance on Jehovah, Jesus faithfully completed the assignment that his Father had entrusted to him! _w 2/1/97_ 16-18a

## Wednesday, January 21

_Husbands ought to be loving their wives as their own bodies. He who loves his wife loves himself._

—Eph. 5:28.

When a man marries, he relinquishes a measure of the freedom he had as a single man that permitted him to be in 'constant attendance upon the Lord without distraction.' (1 Cor. 7:35) Reports show that some married elders have not always been balanced in reasoning on Paul's inspired words. In their desire to accomplish what they feel good elders should do, they may overlook some of their husbandly duties. Certainly, zeal as an elder is commendable. Still, is a Christian balanced if, in discharging his duties in the congregation, he disregards his Scriptural responsibilities toward his wife? Some elders' wives have become spiritually weak, and some have experienced spiritual 'ship-wreck.' (1 Tim. 1:19) While a wife is responsible for working out her own salvation, in some cases the spiritual problem could have been avoided if the elder had 'fed and cherished' his wife, 'as the Christ also does the congregation.'—Eph. 5:29. _w 10/15/96_ 16, 17a

13

<sup>**Page 9**</sup>

## Thursday, January 22

_I am longing to see you, that I may impart some spiritual gift to you in order for you to be made firm; or, rather, that there may be an interchange of encouragement among you.—Rom. 1:11, 12._

When Paul visited the congregations in Macedonia, he 'encouraged them with many a word.' (Acts 20: 1, 2) Words of encouragement can be very helpful in directing both young and old toward spiritual goals. At one large branch office of the Watch Tower Society, an informal survey revealed that circuit overseers had encouraged nearly 20 percent of the volunteers to take up full-time service. The traveling overseer's wife also proves to be a great source of encouragement. Older ones and depressed souls are particularly in need of encouragement. One elderly circuit overseer writes: 'The facet of my work that generates an unspeakable inner joy is the privilege of rendering aid to the inactive and feeble ones among God's flock. I derive immense encouragement and strength while 'imparting some spiritual gift to such ones in order for them to be made firm.'' _w 11/15/96_ 16, 17a

## Friday, January 23

_'Greater will the glory of this later house become than that of the former,' Jehovah of armies has said. 'And in this place I shall give peace.'—Hag. 2:9._

Does Haggai's restoration prophecy have special meaning for later times? It certainly does! The rebuilt temple in Jerusalem became the focus of all true worship on earth. But it prefigured a far more glorious spiritual temple. This began to function in 29 C.E. when, at Jesus' baptism in the Jordan River,

er, Jehovah anointed Jesus as High Priest, holy spirit descending upon him like a dove. (Matt. 3:16) After Jesus had completed his earthly ministry in sacrificial death, he was resurrected to heaven, pictured by the Most Holy of the temple, and there he presented to Jehovah the merit of his sacrifice. This served as a ransom, covering the sins of his disciples, and opened the way for anointing them, on the day of Pentecost 33 C.E., as underpriests in Jehovah's spiritual temple. Their faithful ministry to the death in the temple courtyard on earth would lead to a future heavenly resurrection, for continued priestly service. _w 1/1/97_ 11, 13

## Saturday, January 24

_To do your will, O my God, I have delighted, and your law is within my inward parts.—Ps. 40:8._

As a perfect man on a par with Adam, Jesus was delighted to do the divine will. He was also glad to be held accountable for compliance with Jehovah's law. Concerning him, the psalmist fittingly prophesied the above. Despite the hateful opposition that Jesus experienced, he did God's will and maintained integrity to death on a torture stake. He thereby paid the ransom price to redeem mankind from the death-dealing consequences of Adam's sin. (Matt. 20: 28) Hence, on the eve of his death, Jesus could confidently pray: 'I have glorified you on the earth, having finished the work you have given me to do. So now you, Father, glorify me alongside yourself with the glory that I had alongside you before the world was.' (John 17: 4, 5) Jesus could say those words to his heavenly Father because he was successfully meeting the test of accountability and was acceptable to God. _w 9/15/96_ 6, 7

## Sunday, January 25

_I have become all things to people of all sorts, that I might by all means save some.—1 Cor. 9:22._

People differ. All do not have the same interests or abilities. Some are well educated, while others have very little education. Reading—once a favorite pastime—is now often viewed as a chore. How, then, can we help people with such diverse backgrounds and interests to learn what God requires? A challenging task is easier to perform if you have the proper tools or equipment. A tool that is appropriate for a particular job today may be modified or even replaced tomorrow because of changing needs. It is similar with our commission to proclaim the message about God's Kingdom. Over the years, 'the faithful and discreet slave' has provided us with just the right tools, publications specially designed for use in conducting home Bible studies. (Matt. 24:45) We thus have been equipped to help people of 'all nations and tribes and . . . tongues' to learn God's requirements.—Rev. 7:9. _w 1/15/97_ 6, 7b

## Monday, January 26

_Let us consider one another to incite to love and fine works.—Heb. 10:24._

All of us can benefit by asking: 'Am I upbuilding or critical? Am I balanced or extreme? Do I show consideration for others or insist on my own rights?' A Christian does not try to dictate what steps his brother should or should not take in matters that are not specifically covered in the Bible. (1 Cor. 4:6) In these critical times,

it is important for us to seek ways to encourage one another. To Jehovah, each one is precious. Unhappily, not all feel that way, even about themselves. Many tend to see only their own personal flaws and imperfections. To encourage such ones—and others—could we try to talk to one or two people at each meeting, letting them know why we value their presence and the important contribution they make in the congregation? What a joy to ease their burden in this way and thereby fulfill the law of the Christ!—Gal. 6:2. _w 9/1/96_ 15, 16b

## Tuesday, January 27

_I am giving you a new commandment, that you love one another; just as I have loved you, that you also love one another.—John 13:34._

In harmony with Jesus' new command, we are 'taught by God to love one another.' (1 Thess. 4:9) When things are tranquil and going well, we may feel that we do love all our brothers. Yet what happens when personal differences arise or we become offended because of what another Christian says or does? At this point the genuineness of our love may be put to the test. (Compare 2 Corinthians 8:8.) What does the Bible teach us to do in such situations? One thing is to strive to show love in the fullest sense. (1 Pet. 4:8) Rather than looking for our own interests, getting provoked over minor shortcomings, or keeping an account of an injury, we should work at letting love cover a multitude of sins. (1 Cor. 13:5) We know this is God's will, for it is what his Word teaches. _w 12/15/96_ 15a

14

## Wednesday, January 28

_Better is a dish of vegetables where there is love than a manger-fed bull and hatred along with it.—Prov. 15:17._

Even though Abraham and Sarah went to great efforts to prepare and present a meal to three unknown visitors, note how Abraham earlier referred to it: 'Let me get a piece of bread, and refresh your hearts. Following that, you can pass on, because that is why you have passed this way to your servant.' (Gen. 18:5-8) That 'piece of bread' turned out to be a banquet of fattened calf along with round cakes of fine flour, butter, and milk—a feast fit for a king. What is the point? When hospitality is shown, the important thing, or what is to be emphasized, is not how sumptuous the food and drinks will be, or what elaborate entertainment will be provided, and so on. Hospitality does not depend on whether one can afford expensive things. Rather, it is based on a genuine concern for the welfare of others and on a desire to do good to others to the extent that one can. The above Bible proverb provides the key to genuine hospitality. _w 10/1/96 15_

## Thursday, January 29

_From such a great thing as death he did rescue us and will rescue us; and our hope is in him that he will also rescue us further._

—2 Cor. 1:10.

Some Bible scholars believe that Paul was here referring to the riot in Ephesus, which could have cost him as well as his two Macedonian travelling companions, Gaius and Aristarchus, their lives. (Acts 19:23-41) However, Paul may have been describing a situation far more extreme than that incident. In his

first letter to the Corinthians, Paul asked: 'If, like men, I have fought with wild beasts at Ephesus, of what good is it to me?' (1 Cor. 15:32) This may mean that Paul's life was threatened not just by beastly men but by literal wild animals in the stadium of Ephesus. Criminals were sometimes punished by being forced to fight wild beasts while bloodthirsty crowds looked on. If Paul meant that he had faced literal wild beasts, he must at the last moment have been miraculously spared from a cruel death, just as Daniel was saved from the mouth of literal lions.—Dan. 6:22. _w 11/1/96 13-15a_

## Friday, January 30

_Let the older men who preside in a fine way be reckoned worthy of double honor, especially those who work hard in speaking and teaching.—1 Tim. 5:17._

There are many single men who are using their freedom to serve Kingdom interests. There are also thousands of married brothers without children who, while giving due attention to their wives, serve as fine overseers in districts, circuits, congregations, and Watch Tower branches, with the commendable cooperation of their wives. Finally, in the nearly 80,000 congregations of Jehovah's people, there are many fathers who not only take loving care of their wives and children but also take time to serve their brothers as caring shepherds. (Acts 20:28) Yes, happily, thousands of elders throughout the world are doing a fine job as husbands, fathers, and overseers. Elders who preside in a fine way in their homes and in the congregation deserve our love and respect. We should indeed 'keep holding men of that sort dear.'—Phil. 2:29. _w 10/15/96 16, 18, 19b_

## Saturday, January 31

_I recollect the faith which is in you without any hypocrisy, and which dwelt first in your grandmother Lois and your mother Eunice, but which I am confident is also in you.—2 Tim. 1:5._

If we think of young Timothy as a sapling, he received support from, as it were, two firmly implanted training stakes—his Jewish mother, Eunice, and her mother, Lois, who trained the boy 'from infancy in the holy writings.' (2 Tim. 3:15) Their diligence in teaching Timothy was richly rewarded. Timothy be-

became a missionary in distant lands, perhaps while he was still a teenager, and he had a prominent role in strengthening early Christian congregations. (Acts 16:2-5) Parents, what kind of training stakes are you? For example, do you want your children to develop a balanced view of material things? Then you must set the right example by not pursuing all the latest gadgets or other things that you do not really need. If you choose to pursue material advantages, do not be surprised when your children copy you. (Matt. 6:24; 1 Tim. 6:9, 10) Indeed, if the training stakes are not upright, how can the sapling grow straight? _w 12/1/96 8, 9_

## Sunday, February 1

_Let all the house of Israel know for a certainty that God made him both Lord and Christ, this Jesus whom you impaled._

—Acts 2:36.

If you had been in Jerusalem when Peter spoke to the festival crowd in 33 C.E., what would you have learned about Jesus? Beyond all doubt the miracles he performed showed that he was sent by God. That, although sinful men had put Jesus to death, he was no longer in the grave but had been resurrected and then was exalted to heaven to the right hand of God. That Jesus was, indeed, the Christ, the Messiah of whom the prophets had written. That through Jesus Christ, holy spirit had been poured out on his followers so that they were quickly able to witness to people from many nations about the magnificent things that God was doing by means of his Son. The hearts of many who heard Peter on that occasion were deeply moved, and some 3,000 got baptized as Christian disciples. If you had been there, would you have taken such decisive action?—Acts 2:14-42. _w 2/1/97 6a_

## Monday, February 2

_'The desirable things of all the nations must come in; and I will fill this house with glory,' Jehovah of armies has said._

—Hag. 2:7.

In the days of the prophet Haggai, Jehovah saw to it that all opposition, even an imperial ban, was overcome. Within five years the temple building was brought to grand success. The promise mentioned above was then fulfilled. Those 'desirable things' proved to be non-Israelites who came to worship at that temple, as it reflected the glory of Jehovah's majestic presence. How did this rebuilt temple compare with that constructed in Solomon's day? God's prophet declared: 'Greater will the glory of this later house become than that of the former,' Jehovah of armies has said. (Hag. 2:9) In the initial fulfillment of the prophecy, the rebuilt temple remained longer than the first house. It was still standing when Messiah appeared in 29 C.E. Further, Messiah himself brought it glory when he preached truth there. _w 1/1/97 10, 11_

### Tuesday, February 3

_I am Jehovah your God; and you must sanctify yourselves and you must prove yourselves holy, because I am holy.—Lev. 11:44._

Most of the major religions of the world have those whom they consider to be holy. But what exactly does it mean to be holy? The word “holy” has been defined as being “1. . . . associated with a divine power; sacred. 2. Regarded with or worthy of worship or veneration . . . 3. Living according to a strict or highly moral religious or spiritual system . . . 4. Specified or set apart for a religious purpose.” In the Biblical context, holiness means “religious cleanness or purity; sacredness.” According to _Insight on the Scriptures_, “the original Hebrew \[word] _qo’dhesh_ conveys the thought of separateness, exclusiveness, or sanctification to God, . . . a state of being set aside to the service of God.” The nation of Israel was commanded to be holy. God’s Law stated the above. Today we too must respond to Jehovah’s call to holiness. _w 8/1/96_ 1-3

### Wednesday, February 4

_When he ascended on high he carried away captives; he gave gifts in men.—Eph. 4:8._

Traveling overseers and other elders favored with assignments of service by God’s undeserved kindness are called “gifts in men.” As Jehovah’s representative and Head of the congregation, Jesus has provided these spiritual men in order that we might be built up individually and attain to maturity. (Eph. 4: 8-15) Any gift deserves an expression of appreciation. Especially is this true of a gift that fortifies us to keep serving Jehovah. So, then,

when the circuit overseer’s visit is announced, we can start making plans to have a full share in the congregation’s activities for that week. Perhaps we can set aside extra time to support the field service arrangements during the visit. We may be able to serve as auxiliary pioneers during that month. Surely we will want to put the circuit overseer’s suggestions into practice in order to improve our ministry. Such a receptive spirit will benefit us and will reassure him that his visit is a useful one. _w 11/15/96_ 8, 9

### Thursday, February 5

_Necessity is laid upon me. Really, woe is me if I did not declare the good news!—1 Cor. 9:16._

Jehovah has good news for mankind. He has a Kingdom, and he wants people everywhere to hear about it! Once we learn this good news, God requires that we share it with others. This is a twofold work. First, we must proclaim the good news of God’s Kingdom. Jesus said: “This good news of the kingdom will be preached in all the inhabited earth for a witness to all the nations; and then the end will come.” (Matt. 24:14) The second aspect of this work involves teaching those who respond favorably to the Kingdom proclamation. After his resurrection, Jesus told a large group of his disciples: “Go therefore and make disciples of people of all the nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the holy spirit, teaching them to observe all the things I have commanded you.” (Matt. 28:19, 20) This involves helping others to learn God’s requirements. Honesthearted people must meet God’s requirements in order to become subjects of his Kingdom. _w 1/15/97_ 1, 2b

### Friday, February 6

_When you catch sight of the disgusting thing that causes desolation standing where it ought not (let the reader use discernment), then let those in Judea begin fleeing to the mountains._

—Mark 13:14.

How did that prophecy prove accurate in its first fulfillment? _The International Standard Bible Encyclopedia_ (1982) tells us: “The Jews were increasingly restive under Roman control and the procurators were increasingly violent, cruel, and dishonest. Open rebellion broke out in A.D. 66.” The Roman Twelfth Legion under Cestius Gallus marched from Syria, ravaged Galilee and Judea, and then attacked the capital, even occupying the upper section of Jerusalem. Historian Flavius Josephus relates what followed: “Cestius \[Gallus], aware of neither the despair of the besieged nor the feelings of the people, suddenly called off his men.” (_The Jewish War_, II, 540 \[xix, 7]) Why did Gallus retreat? Whatever the reason, his retreat allowed Christians to obey Jesus’ command and flee to the mountains and to safety. Obedience was lifesaving. _w 8/15/96_ 4-8a

### Saturday, February 7

_Remember, now, your Grand Creator in the days of your young manhood.—Eccl. 12:1._

Happily, many youths, past and present, have remembered their Creator. Little Samuel remained steadfast in tabernacle service despite immoral influences. (1 Sam. 2: 12-26) An alluring temptress, Potiphar’s wife, could not entice young Joseph to commit fornication. (Gen. 39:1-12) Even though “but a boy,” Jeremiah preached courageously in the face of severe opposition.

(Jer. 1:6-8) A little Israelite girl fearlessly directed a powerful Syrian army chief to seek help in Israel, where he could learn about Jehovah. (2 Ki. 5:1-4) Young Daniel and his companions kept their faith when tested on dietary laws of God. And the youths Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego chose to be hurled into a fiery furnace rather than compromise their loyalty to God. Today more than 2,000 youths 19 to 25 years of age serve at the world headquarters of Jehovah’s Witnesses. They are only a small portion of the tens of thousands of young ones who are remembering their Creator worldwide.—Dan. 1: 8, 17; 3:16-18. _w 12/1/96_ 19, 20a

### Sunday, February 8

_Ezra himself had prepared his heart to consult the law of Jehovah and to do it and to teach in Israel regulation and justice._

—Ezra 7:10.

Ezra fought hard against the corrupting influences of other nations and stressed the reading and teaching of the Law. (Neh. 8:5-8) Some teachers of the Law claimed to follow in Ezra’s footsteps and formed what came to be termed the “Great Synagogue.” Among its sayings was the directive: “Make a fence around the Law.” These teachers reasoned that the Law was like a precious garden. In order that no one should trespass in this garden by transgressing its laws, they created further laws, the “Oral Law.” By 200 B.C.E., the oral law was making its way into Jewish life. At first these laws were not to be written, lest they be taken as equal to the written Law. But gradually, human thinking was put ahead of the divine, so that eventually this “fence” actually damaged the very “garden” it was supposed to protect. _w 9/1/96_ 11, 12

## Monday, February 9

_They have said: 'The time has not come, the time of the house of Jehovah, for it to be built.'_

—Hag. 1:2.

No longer was temple construction, representing the restoration of divine worship, the first concern of the Israelites who had returned from exile. They had turned to building palatial homes for themselves. A materialistic outlook had diminished their enthusiasm for Jehovah's worship. As a result, his blessing had been removed. Their fields were no longer fruitful, and they lacked clothing for the harsh wintertime. Their income had become meager, and it was as though they were putting money in a bag full of holes. (Hag. 1:3-6) Twice, Jehovah gave the strong admonition: 'Set your heart upon your ways.' Evidently, Zerubbabel, the governor of Jerusalem, and high priest Joshua responded and valiantly encouraged all the people 'to listen to the voice of Jehovah their God, and to the words of Haggai the prophet, as Jehovah their God had sent him; and the people began to fear because of Jehovah.' Moreover, Haggai reassured them, saying: 'I am with you people,' is the utterance of Jehovah.'—Hag. 1:5, 7-14. _w 1/1/97_ 8, 9

## Tuesday, February 10

_I wish all men were as I myself am. Nevertheless, each one has his own gift from God, one in this way, another in that way._

—1 Cor. 7:7.

Paul showed that both marriage and singleness are gifts from God. He doubtless had in mind what Jesus said. After establishing that marriage came from God, Jesus showed that willing singleness for

the sake of serving Kingdom interests is a particular gift: 'Not all men make room for the saying, but only those who have the gift. For . . . there are eunuchs that have made themselves eunuchs on account of the kingdom of the heavens. Let him that can make room for it make room for it.' (Matt. 19: 11, 12) Jehovah's holy spirit was undoubtedly guiding Paul when he gave counsel on the subject. His whole presentation of celibacy and marriage shows balance and restraint. He does not make it a matter of faithfulness or unfaithfulness. It is, rather, a question of free choice, with the balance tipping in favor of singleness for those who are able to remain chaste in that state. _w 10/15/96_ 7, 9

## Wednesday, February 11

_In showing honor to one another take the lead._—Rom. 12:10.

Abraham truly demonstrated the meaning of that statement. At Genesis 18:1-10, we read that three strangers came to Abraham's camp at Mamre. The account tells us that after meeting the strangers, Abraham 'proceeded to bow down to the earth.' Bowing to perfect strangers? Well, a bow, as performed by Abraham, was a way of greeting an honored guest or someone of high position, not to be confused with an act of worship, which is reserved only for God. (Compare Acts 10: 25, 26; Revelation 19:10.) By bowing, not just a tipping of the head but a bowing 'down to the earth,' Abraham accorded these strangers the honor of being important. He was the head of a large, prosperous patriarchal family, yet he regarded these strangers as worthy of greater honor than himself. How different this is from the customary suspicion of strangers, the wait-and-see attitude! _w 10/1/96_ 13

## Thursday, February 12

_Having a tender affection for you, we were well pleased to impart to you, not only the good news of God, but also our own souls, because you became beloved to us._—1 Thess. 2:8.

Traveling overseers are constantly on the move. This in itself requires a self-sacrificing spirit. Traveling from one congregation to another can often be difficult, but these men and their wives do so with a joyful attitude. Like Paul's companion Timothy, they sometimes have health problems. (1 Tim. 5:23) This places extra stress on them. But despite health problems and other trials, traveling overseers and their wives find joy in their service and display self-sacrificing love. Some have risked their lives to render spiritual help in times of persecution or warfare. When visiting congregations, they have manifested a spirit similar to that of Paul, who told Thessalonian Christians the above. _w 11/15/96_ 3-5

## Friday, February 13

_I will request the Father and he will give you another comforter to be with you forever._

—John 14:16, footnote.

On the night before his death, Jesus made it clear to his faithful apostles that he would soon be leaving them and returning to his Father. This troubled and grieved them. (John 13:33, 36; 14:27-31) Recognizing their need for continued comfort, Jesus promised the above. Jesus here referred to God's holy spirit, which was poured out upon his disciples 50 days after his resurrection. Among other things, God's spirit comforted them during their trials and strengthened them to continue doing God's will.

(Acts 4:31) Jesus also became 'sorely troubled' and 'deeply grieved' on the night before his agonizing death. (Matt. 26:37, 38) So he prayed to his Father for help. The Bible reports that 'an angel from heaven appeared to \[Jesus] and strengthened him.' (Luke 22:43) The courageous and manly way in which Jesus went on to face his opposers is evidence that God's way of comforting his Son was most effective.—John 18:3-8, 33-38. _w 11/1/96_ 14, 17

## Saturday, February 14

_To this course you were called, because even Christ suffered for you, leaving you a model for you to follow his steps closely._

—1 Pet. 2:21.

The way that Jesus dealt with people who were ill or disabled gives evidence of his warmth and compassion as well as of his ability to bring them relief. Once, a diseased man filled with leprosy approached him and pleaded: 'Lord, if you just want to, you can make me clean.' Without hesitation, Jesus reached out and touched the leprosy man, saying: 'I want to. Be made clean.' (Matt. 8:2, 3) On another occasion a woman sought to be cured by inconspicuously touching the fringe of his garment. Jesus dealt with her in a kindly and reassuring manner. (Luke 8:43-48) And when he encountered a funeral procession, he was moved with pity for the grieving widow whose only son had died. He resurrected that dead man and restored him to his mother. (Luke 4:2-4; 7:11-16) As we read these accounts and meditate on the spirit Jesus displayed, our love is deepened for this one who laid down his human life so that we might live forever. Even though we have never seen him, we feel drawn to him, and we want to follow in his footsteps. _w 2/1/97_ 14, 15a

20

<sup>**Page 13**</sup>

[Open Page 13 of PDF](https://pdfs-noindex.themindscapeishere.workers.dev/1998_es_E.pdf#page=13)

## Sunday, February 15

_The love of the Christ compels us._
—2 Cor. 5:14, footnote.

The law of the Christ includes all of Christ Jesus' commands—whether preaching and teaching, keeping the eye pure and simple, working at keeping peace with our neighbor, or removing uncleanness from the congregation. Yet, there is more. Jehovah's organization, as well as individual congregations, has to establish necessary rules and procedures in order to preserve good order. (1 Cor. 14:33, 40) Why, Christians could not even meet together if they had no rules as to when, where, and how to hold such meetings! Cooperating with reasonable guidelines laid down by those given authority in the organization is also a part of fulfilling the law of the Christ. (Heb. 13:17) Nonetheless, true Christians do not allow their worship to become a meaningless structure of laws. They do not serve Jehovah merely because some individual or organization tells them to do so. Rather, the driving force behind their worship is love, as Paul stated above. Self-sacrificing love is the basis of the law of the Christ. _w 9/1/96_ 3, 4b

## Monday, February 16

_Out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks._
—Matt. 12:34.

Jesus clearly taught that what is in the heart will become evident by what a person says and does. He also showed that wicked actions spring from wicked thoughts in the heart, or inner person. He said: "The things proceeding out of the mouth come out of the heart, and those things defile a man." (Matt. 15:18-20) This helps us to understand that unholy actions are not just spontaneous or without a prior

basis. They are the result of defiling thoughts that have been lurking in the heart—secret desires and maybe fantasies. That is why Jesus could say: "I say to you that everyone that keeps on looking at a woman so as to have a passion for her has already committed adultery with her in his heart." (Matt. 5:28) In other words, fornication and adultery have already taken root in the heart before any action takes place. Then, given the right circumstances, the unholy thoughts become unholy conduct. _w 8/1/96_ 11, 12

## Tuesday, February 17

_Now concerning the things about which you wrote, it is well for a man not to touch a woman._
—1 Cor. 7:1.

The expression "not to touch a woman" here means avoiding physical contact with a woman for sexual gratification. Paul was here recommending the single state. Why? Well, likely the Christians in Corinth had come into contact with Greek philosophy, of which certain schools lauded extreme asceticism, or self-denial. Might that have been why the Corinthians asked Paul if it would be "well" for Christians to avoid all sexual intercourse? Paul's answer did not reflect Greek philosophy. (Col. 2:8) Unlike Catholic theologians, he nowhere recommended a celibate ascetic life in a monastery or convent, as if single persons were particularly holy and could contribute to their own salvation by their life-style and prayers. Paul recommended singleness "in view of the necessity here with us." He might have been referring to the difficult times Christians were going through, which could be compounded by marriage. His counsel to unmarried Christians was: "It is well for them that they remain even as I am."—1 Cor. 7:8, 26, 28. _w 10/15/96_ 4-6

## Wednesday, February 18

_Why do you judge your brother? Or why do you also look down on your brother? For we shall all stand before the judgment seat of God._—Rom. 14:10.

Unlike the perfect man Jesus Christ, we are imperfect. Yet, we are accountable to God, as the apostle Paul stated above. In order that we may render an account and find acceptance with Jehovah, he has lovingly given us both a conscience and his inspired Word, the Bible, to guide us in what we say and do. (Rom. 2:14, 15; 2 Tim. 3:16, 17) Taking full advantage of Jehovah's spiritual provisions and following our Bible-trained conscience will help us to find acceptance with God. Jehovah's holy spirit, or active force, is an added source of strength and guidance. If we act in harmony with the spirit's direction and the leadings of our Bible-trained conscience, we show that we do not 'disregard God,' to whom we must account for all our actions.—1 Thess. 4:3-8. _w 9/15/96_ 8

## Thursday, February 19

_Keep strict watch that how you walk is not as unwise but as wise persons, buying out the opportune time for yourselves, because the days are wicked. On this account cease becoming unreasonable, but go on perceiving what the will of Jehovah is._
—Eph. 5:15-17.

Families are encouraged to make a habit of considering the daily text from _Examining the Scriptures Daily_. Just reading the text and comments is good, but discussing the text and applying it is more beneficial. For example, if considering the above text, family mem-

bers could reason on how to 'buy out opportune time' for personal study, to share in some form of the full-time ministry, and to care for other theocratic assignments. Yes, a family discussion of the daily text could lead one or many to "go on perceiving \[more fully] what the will of Jehovah is." Parents must be assiduous teachers of their children. (Deut. 6:6, 7) But it is not simply a matter of lecturing or commanding their offspring. Father and mother also need to listen, in that way being better able to know what needs to be explained, clarified, illustrated, or repeated. _w 12/15/96_ 13, 14a

## Friday, February 20

_To be sure, you yourselves know, brothers, how our visit to you has not been without results._
—1 Thess. 2:1.

When the traveling overseer and his wife set zealous examples in the evangelizing work, elders, their wives, and the rest of the congregation are encouraged to display zeal in their ministry. "Be zealous for the service," urged one circuit overseer. He added: "I have found that, generally, the more zealous a congregation is in the ministry, the fewer problems they experience." Another circuit overseer remarked: "I believe that if the elders work in the field with the brothers and sisters and help them to enjoy the ministry, this will result in peace of mind and the greatest satisfaction in serving Jehovah." The apostle Paul 'mustered up boldness to speak the good news of God to the Thessalonians with a great deal of struggling.' No wonder they had fond memories of his visit and preaching activity and longed to see him again!—1 Thess. 2:2; 3:6. _w 11/15/96_ 12a

## Saturday, February 21

_The conclusion of the matter, everything having been heard, is: Fear the true God and keep his commandments. For this is the whole obligation of man._
—Eccl. 12:13.

Suppose you owned a beautiful home, one that you recently renovated at great cost. Would you allow just anyone to live there? Of course not! Any potential tenant would have to meet your requirements. Similarly, Jehovah God has provided this earthly home for the human family. Under the rule of his Kingdom, the earth will soon be "renovated"—transformed into a beautiful paradise. Jehovah will accomplish this. At great cost to himself, he gave his only-begotten Son to make it possible. Surely, God must have requirements for those who will live there! (Matt. 6:9, 10; John 3:16) How can we find out what God's requirements are? Jehovah inspired wise King Solomon to sum up what He expects of us. After reflecting on all that he had pursued—including wealth, building projects, musical interests, and romantic love—Solomon came to realize the above. w 1/15/97 1-3a

## Sunday, February 22

_Daughter, your faith has made you well._—Mark 5:34.

Jesus was practical and addressed the real needs and concerns of the people. Rather than adding more regulations to the Mosaic Law, Jesus showed how the Jews should have been applying that Law all along—with reasonableness and mercy. Recall, for example, the occasion when he was approached by a woman afflicted with a flow of blood. According to the Mosaic Law, anyone she

touched would become unclean, so she was certainly not supposed to mix with a crowd of people! (Lev. 15:25-27) But she was so desperate to be healed that she made her way through the crowd and touched Jesus' outer garment. The bleeding stopped immediately. Did he rebuke her for violating the Law? No; instead, he understood her desperate circumstance and demonstrated the Law's greatest precept—love. Empathetically he told her the above and added: "Go in peace, and be in good health from your grievous sickness."—Mark 5:25-34. w 9/1/96 10, 11a

## Monday, February 23

_I, Jehovah, am . . . the One saying of Cyrus, "He is my shepherd, and all that I delight in he will completely carry out"; even in my saying of Jerusalem, "She will be rebuilt," and of the temple, "You will have your foundation laid."—Isa. 44:24, 28._

Who was this Cyrus? Remarkably, he proved to be King Cyrus of Persia, who conquered Babylon in 539 B.C.E. True to Jehovah's words recorded by Isaiah, Cyrus issued to captive Israel the decree: "Whoever there is among you of all his people, may his God prove to be with him. So let him go up to Jerusalem, which is in Judah, and rebuild the house of Jehovah the God of Israel—he is the true God—which was in Jerusalem." A supremely happy Jewish remnant along with the non-Israelite Nethinim and the sons of the servants of Solomon returned to Jerusalem. They arrived in time to celebrate the Festival of Booths in 537 B.C.E. and make sacrifices to Jehovah at his altar. The next year, in the second month, they laid the foundation of the second temple. —Ezra 1:1-4. w 1/1/97 2, 3

## Tuesday, February 24

_Borrow money and you are the lender's slave._—Prov. 22:7, "Today's English Version."

If you fall into debt, it can be said that you have another master. Wisely, therefore, you will try to live by the Scriptural principle 'not to be owing anybody a single thing, except to love one another.' (Rom. 13:8) Heeding the exhortation, "Remember, now, your Grand Creator," is a key to the wise management of your finances. (Eccl. 12:1) This is because when you obey that command, your priority is to please Jehovah, and your affection for him influences how you spend your money. Accordingly, you will try not to allow personal wants to interfere with giving God whole-souled devotion. (Matt. 16:24-26) You will strive to keep your eye "simple," that is, clearly focused on God's Kingdom and the doing of his will. (Matt. 6:22-24) You will thus come to view the divine admonition to "honor Jehovah with your valuable things" as a joyful privilege.—Prov. 3:9. w 12/1/96 16-18a

## Wednesday, February 25

_God recommends his own love to us in that, while we were yet sinners, Christ died for us. . . . When we were enemies, we became reconciled to God through the death of his Son._—Rom. 5:8, 10.

In addition to providing abundantly for the material needs of his creatures, Jehovah cares for their needs in a spiritual way, as we read above. That provision makes it possible for sinful humans to come into a happy family relationship with our heavenly Father. (Rom. 8:20, 21) Jehovah also saw to it that we are provided with proper guidance and direction so that we can make a success of life in spite

of our sinful and imperfect state. (Ps. 119:105; 2 Tim. 3:16) As a God of such loving-kindness and generosity, Jehovah wants his worshipers to imitate him. The _Encyclopaedia Judaica_ observes that "in ancient Israel, hospitality was not merely a question of good manners, but a moral institution." More than being a trademark of any particular nationality or ethnic group, hospitality should be a characteristic of all true worshipers of Jehovah. w 10/1/96 8, 10

## Thursday, February 26

_The ten horns that you saw, and the wild beast, these will hate the harlot and will make her devastated and naked, and will eat up her fleshy parts and will completely burn her with fire._

—Rev. 17:16.

According to the context of the above, God will put it into the heart of the political element to attack Babylon the Great, the world empire of false religion. As this devastating attack on religion advances, it might seem that it will wipe out all religious organizations, including Jehovah's people. This is where Jesus' words found at Matthew 24:22 will be fulfilled on a large scale. As the chosen ones in Jerusalem appeared to be at risk, Jehovah's servants might seem in danger of being eliminated during the attack on religion, as if that attack would wipe out all "flesh" of God's people. Yet, back in 66 C.E., the tribulation caused by the Romans was cut short, allowing God's anointed chosen ones ample opportunity to escape and remain alive. Thus, we can rest assured that the destructive attack on religion will not be allowed to kill off the global congregation of true worshipers. Somehow it will be cut short so that God's people can be "saved." w 8/15/96 17, 18a

## Friday, February 27

_Each of us will render an account for himself to God.—Rom. 14:12._

Jehovah God created our first parents, Adam and Eve, as free moral agents. Though they were lower than the angels, they were intelligent creatures capable of making wise decisions. (Ps. 8:4, 5) Yet, that God-given freedom was not a license to exercise self-determination. They were accountable to their Creator, and this accountability has extended to all their descendants. Now that we are nearing the climax of this wicked system of things, Jehovah will make an accounting on the earth. (Compare Romans 9:28.) Soon, ungodly men will have to render an account to Jehovah God for the despilting of earth's resources, the destruction of human life, and especially the persecution of his servants. (Rev. 6:10; 11:18) Faced with this sobering prospect, it is essential for us to reflect on Jehovah's righteous dealings with his creatures in times past and see how the Scriptures can help us to render an acceptable account to our Creator. _w 9/15/96 1-3_

## Sunday, March 1

_When the Son of man arrives in his glory, . . . all the nations will be gathered before him, and he will separate people one from another, just as a shepherd separates the sheep from the goats.—Matt. 25:31, 32._

As we await the great day of Jehovah, doubtless we desire to do all we can in his service. At God's appointed time, Jesus will render judgment and separate righteous 'sheep' worthy of everlasting

## Saturday, February 28

_Paul, an apostle of Christ Jesus through God's will, to the holy ones who are in Ephesus and faithful ones in union with Christ Jesus.—Eph. 1:1._

What if you had been associated with the Christian congregation in Ephesus when Paul's inspired letter to the disciples there was received? What could you have learned from it about Jesus' role in God's purpose? In that letter Paul explained that through Christ all things in heaven and on earth would be brought back into harmony with God, that God's gift through Christ extended to people of all nations, that individuals who had been dead in God's sight because of their trespasses were being made alive through faith in Christ, and that it was again possible for humans to become beloved sons of God. (Eph. 1:5-10; 2:4, 5, 11-13) Would appreciation for all of this have deepened your love for God's Son? Out of love for God and gratitude to his Son, would you have made needed adjustments so that the doing of God's will was truly the focal point of your life?—Eph. 5:15-17. _w 2/1/97 8, 9a_

life from ungodly 'goats' deserving eternal destruction. (John 5:22) We are not the ones who separate the sheep from the goats. But our service as preachers of righteousness is affording people the opportunity to choose a life of service to God and thus gain the hope of being separated to life when Jesus 'arrives in his glory.' The shortness of the time left for this system of things heightens the need for wholehearted activity as we search for those 'rightly disposed for everlasting life.'—Acts 13:48. _w 3/1/97 15a_

26

## Monday, March 2

_In the region of light they must glorify Jehovah.—Isa. 24:15._

Jehovah—the illustrious name of God! How the faithful prophets of old rejoiced to speak in that name! With exultation they glorified their Sovereign Lord, Jehovah, whose name identifies him as the Great Purposer. (Isa. 40:5; Jer. 10:6, 10; Ezek. 36:23) Even the so-called minor prophets were most vocal in giving glory to Jehovah. One of these was Haggai. In the book of Haggai, made up of just 38 verses, God's name is used 35 times. Such prophecy sounds lifeless when the precious name Jehovah is replaced by the title 'Lord,' as the superfine apostles of Christendom render it in their Bible translations. (Compare 2 Corinthians 11:5.) At Isaiah 12:2, a double form of the name is used. The prophet declares: 'Look! God is my salvation. I shall trust and be in no dread; for Jah Jehovah is my strength and my might, and he came to be the salvation of me.' (See also Isaiah 26:4.) Thus, some 200 years before Israel's release from Babylonish captivity, Jah Jehovah through his prophet Isaiah was giving assurance that he was their mighty Savior. _w 1/1/97 1, 2_

## Tuesday, March 3

_Go on carrying the burdens of one another, and thus fulfill the law of the Christ.—Gal. 6:2._

In Rwanda, Hutu and Tutsi Witnesses of Jehovah risked their lives to protect one another from the ethnic slaughter that recently swept that land. Jehovah's Witnesses in Kobe, Japan, who lost family members in the devastating earthquake were shattered by their loss. Yet, they moved promptly to rescue other victims. Yes, heart-

warming examples from around the world demonstrate that the law of the Christ is at work today. It is a tremendous force for good. By contrast, Christendom's religion is often a matter of form, not of the heart. Is that because it is too difficult to live by the law of the Christ? No. Jesus would not give us a law that could not be followed. Christendom has simply missed the point of that law. She has failed to heed the inspired words stated above. We 'fulfill the law of the Christ' by carrying one another's burdens, not by imitating the Pharisees and unjustifiably adding to our brothers' loads. _w 9/1/96 1, 2b_

## Wednesday, March 4

_Out of much tribulation and anguish of heart I wrote you with many tears.—2 Cor. 2:4._

The apostle Paul's ministry in Ephesus was marked by 'tears and trials that befell \[him] by the plots of the Jews.' (Acts 20:17-20) Finally, Paul left Ephesus after supporters of the goddess Artemis had got the city in an uproar over his preaching activity. (Acts 19:23-29; 20:1) As Paul headed north to the city of Troas, something else weighed heavily on his mind. The young congregation in Corinth was plagued by division, and it was tolerating fornication. So from Ephesus, Paul had written a letter of strong reproof in hopes of correcting the situation. This was not an easy thing for him to do, as he later revealed in a second letter, where we read the words of today's text. Like Paul, compassionate elders do not find it easy to give corrective counsel and reproof, in part because they are only too aware of their own weaknesses. (Gal. 6:1) May we, then, be of comfort to those taking the lead among us by readily responding to loving, Bible-based counsel.—Heb. 13:17. _w 11/1/96 18_
